Transcript intake and student record\\r\\n\\r\\nThis is an internal procedure for `post-session-recap`. It covers source retrieval, the primary student record, and private follow-ups. A request only to find or inspect a transcript stops at that narrower task.\\r\\n\\r\\nAfter every session Joshua wants a durable set of lecture notes. The student should be\\r\\nable to return several days later, understand the lesson without remembering a specific\\r\\nquestion, and apply the method to new work. It is teaching content, not an email or a\\r\\nsession summary.\\r\\n\\r\\nRead `skills/reference/session-recap-quality-reference.md` before drafting. Its Sofia\\r\\nnote is the quality bar for depth, hierarchy, nuance, and practical usefulness. Copy the\\r\\nmethod, not the conditional-logic headings.\\r\\n\\r\\nDraft and file the note by default. If Joshua explicitly says to post, assign, publish,\\r\\nor send it to the student, complete that as a separate LSAT U platform action after the\\r\\ndraft is ready and verify what landed. Never infer posting permission from a request to\\r\\nwrite a note.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 1. Get the transcript on disk first\\r\\n\\r\\nTranscripts come from Google Meet\'s Gemini notes. The calendar event carries the Drive\\r\\ndoc as an attachment; some sessions have the doc in Drive but never got it attached.\\r\\nCheck both.\\r\\n\\r\\nUse `gws calendar events list` on the primary calendar for the date window. Match the student\'s guest email, never just the event title. Then use `gws drive files list` for native Docs whose name contains `Notes by Gemini` in the same window. Include unattached Docs and untitled events named `Meeting started ...`. Export each matching native Doc with `gws drive files export` and MIME type `text/plain`; Markdown export expands and escapes the transcript. Build the command\'s JSON `--params` argument for the active shell rather than copying Bash quoting into PowerShell.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe Drive result can include Markdown copies uploaded by an earlier intake. Count native\\r\\nGoogle Docs as source transcripts and use the file ID to deduplicate. Do not count an\\r\\nuploaded `YYYY-MM-DD-<slug>-notes-by-gemini.md` copy as another session. Reconcile the\\r\\nfull week against Calendar before exporting so unattached docs and events with no doc\\r\\nboth remain visible in the report.\\r\\n\\r\\nFile to `students/<slug>/transcript-downloads/YYYY-MM-DD-<slug>-notes-by-gemini.md`.\\r\\nResolve the person with `students/_aliases.md`; for a \\"Meeting started\\" doc, count\\r\\nspeaker labels in the transcript (use `rg -o \\"^[A-Z][a-z]+( [A-Z][a-z]+)?:\\"` and count the labels).\\r\\nConsultations and people without a folder go to `students/_other-sessions/`.\\r\\n**Never create a student folder without asking.** Skip anything already on disk.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe Google Classroom calendars (`c_classroom...`) have been empty since 2026; the\\r\\nprimary calendar holds every session.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 2. Read the whole transcript, not the Gemini summary\\r\\n\\r\\nThe Gemini \\"Quick notes\\" flatten what he said. Read the transcript section. It is 40\\r\\nto 90 KB; that is the cost of a recap that is true.\\r\\n\\r\\nExtract:\\r\\n\\r\\n- the governing concept and distinctions Joshua taught;\\r\\n- the student\'s actual confusion or decision point;\\r\\n- the reasoning procedure, including why each step works;\\r\\n- legal and illegal moves, edge cases, diagnostic checks, and shortcuts;\\r\\n- examples that can stand alone outside the original question; and\\r\\n- the exact practice and follow-ups that were assigned.\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not write a play-by-play of answer choices. A detail from a specific question belongs\\r\\nonly when it demonstrates a reusable move. If the explanation needs more support, search\\r\\nJoshua\'s corpus first. Use an authoritative outside source only when Joshua points to it\\r\\nor the corpus cannot resolve the concept. Keep source research outside the student note.\\r\\nThe session transcript remains the authority for what happened and what was assigned.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 3. Write the recap\\r\\n\\r\\nPath: `students/<slug>/recaps/YYYY-MM-DD-recap.md`. One file per session. Primary\\r\\nrecord: never regenerate over an existing one; a redo gets a dated correction section.\\r\\n\\r\\n```markdown\\r\\n---\\r\\ntitle: Recap · <Student> · YYYY-MM-DD\\r\\ntype: recap\\r\\nrecord_kind: primary\\r\\nstudent: <slug>\\r\\nsession_date: YYYY-MM-DD\\r\\nsource: transcript-downloads/YYYY-MM-DD-<slug>-notes-by-gemini.md\\r\\nstatus: draft\\r\\nposted: false\\r\\nposted_at:\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# <Concept the student needs>\\r\\n\\r\\n<Open directly with the governing distinction or decision. One short orientation\\r\\nparagraph. No greeting, sign-off, praise, generic scene-setting, \\"Today...\\", or recap of\\r\\nwhich answers were correct.>\\r\\n\\r\\n## <A descriptive header that teaches the first part of the method>\\r\\n\\r\\n<Define the idea before applying it. Build a reusable reference with the combination of\\r\\nshort explanation, numbered steps, a compact table, or a spaced example that best fits\\r\\nthe material.>\\r\\n\\r\\n## <The next conceptual move>\\r\\n\\r\\n<Explain why it works, how to recognize when to use it, what does not follow, and how\\r\\nthe student can check the move. Add boundary cases or common traps when they mattered in\\r\\nthe session.>\\r\\n\\r\\n## <A repeatable process>\\r\\n\\r\\n1. <A decision the student can actually make.>\\r\\n2. <The next observable check.>\\r\\n3. <How to verify the inference or reject the trap.>\\r\\n\\r\\n## Before next session\\r\\n\\r\\n- [ ] <What was actually assigned, as the student would recognise it.>\\r\\n- [ ] <Only what the transcript supports. No invented drills.>\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Notes for me (not posted)\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Weak spots:** <tags>\\r\\n- **Follow-ups on my side:** <things Joshua promised: send materials, assign a drill, schedule>\\r\\n- **Watch:** <a teaching judgment worth carrying into teaching-notes.md if it repeats>\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nEverything above the `---` rule is the student note and must stand alone when pasted.\\r\\nEverything below is admin-only.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe headings above are scaffolding, not a mandatory outline. Organize around the lesson\'s\\r\\nlogic. Use enough depth to teach the method fully, but remove any section that does not\\r\\nhelp the student use it.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Voice rules (from `wiki/teaching-voice.md`, Coaching register)\\r\\n\\r\\n- A post-session note is a set of lecture notes, not an email. Use a concept title,\\r\\n  descriptive headers, numbered procedures, and spaced examples. Do not use a greeting\\r\\n  or sign-off.\\r\\n- Open with Joshua\'s concrete explanation of the concept. Define the words the student\\r\\n  needs in plain language before diagrams or inference rules. Do not invent abstract\\r\\n  umbrella wording such as “how much of a group fits a description” when the direct\\r\\n  definition is “some means at least one; most means more than half; all means every one.”\\r\\n- Short sentences. One idea per line. Air between ideas.\\r\\n- Write for a student returning days later. Do not rely on \\"remember that question\\" or\\r\\n  unexplained references to a person, scenario, or answer choice.\\r\\n- Teach the reasoning; never just state the answer. \\"A reverses the timeline: the\\r\\n  symptom stops, then the boss assumes the cause was fixed\\" beats \\"A was wrong\\".\\r\\n- Praise only when it names a durable skill or meaningful change and ties to their\\r\\n  history. Never praise the mere fact that an answer was correct.\\r\\n- Honest about the test, never blunt about the student. When it slipped, say it is a\\r\\n  normal place to slip and move to the fix.\\r\\n- **No em dashes, ever.** Periods, colons, the middot (·).\\r\\n- No \\"unlock\\", \\"dive deep\\", \\"elevate\\", \\"game-changer\\", \\"seamless\\", \\"robust\\", no emoji.\\r\\n- No internal vocabulary: no \\"beat\\", no \\"door\\", no tool or model names, no \\"transcript\\"\\r\\n  or \\"Gemini\\" in the student note.\\r\\n- Only what the transcript supports. If he did not assign it, it is not in \\"Before\\r\\n  next session\\".\\r\\n- Read `wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md` before writing; every past wording fix is there.\\r\\n- For conditional LSAT notes, distinguish an asserted fact from a general rule. A\\r\\n  rule states what follows if its trigger is present; it does not establish that the\\r\\n  trigger occurred. Some stimuli contain rules and no facts. Never invent a fact to\\r\\n  trigger a rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 4. Validate, log, and hand back\\r\\n\\r\\nBefore handoff, check that:\\r\\n\\r\\n- the opening begins with content rather than commentary about the session;\\r\\n- the note explains both the move and the reason it is valid;\\r\\n- examples make sense without the original question;\\r\\n- hierarchy is visible at a glance;\\r\\n- practice matches the transcript; and\\r\\n- the student-facing portion contains no private context, transcript references, or\\r\\n  administrative follow-ups.\\r\\n\\r\\n- Append the session line to the student\'s `transcripts.md` if one exists (primary,\\r\\n  append-only), and any promise he made to `open-loops.md`.\\r\\n- Append one line to `wiki/log.md`: `## YYYY-MM-DD recap | <students>` with paths.\\r\\n- Report: which recaps were written, which sessions had no transcript (list them, with\\r\\n  the reason if known), and anything that needs his call (a new name, a folder to\\r\\n  create). If Joshua explicitly requested a platform action, report its verification\\r\\n  separately from the draft.\\r\\n"}\n{"chunk_id":"38171a","wall_time_seconds":1.8491140000000001,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":7309,"output":"---\\r\\ntitle: Teaching Voice\\r\\ntype: concept\\r\\ntags: [lsat-u, voice, pedagogy, content]\\r\\ncreated: 2026-06-10\\r\\nupdated: 2026-07-18\\r\\nstatus: active\\r\\nsubject: Teaching Voice\\r\\naliases: []\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Teaching Voice\\r\\n\\r\\nHow LSAT U sounds, across every surface. This is the canonical voice doc: route all student-facing and brand copy through it. Built from a grill with Joshua (2026-06-13), then tested and enriched against the full corpus of his real correspondence (944 messages he authored, mid-2025 to mid-2026, from the Gmail takeout; 2026-07-18). Claims below marked with a quote are evidenced by that corpus.\\r\\n\\r\\nIt replaces the old two-register / 15-named-tools \\"preserve verbatim\\" profile (deleted 2026-06-10) and folds in the brand/marketing register the earlier draft was missing.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Core voice (every register)\\r\\n\\r\\nThe constant under all three registers below. This is Joshua on the page.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Quiet authority.** State the value and let it land. Never oversell, never beg, never perform enthusiasm.\\r\\n- **Specific, never generic.** Real scores, the actual skill, the student\'s own history. Praise is a specific observation, not \\"great job.\\"\\r\\n- **Honest about the work, gentle about the person.** Be blunt that the LSAT is hard (\\"expect this to be exhausting and very challenging\\"). Never be blunt about a student\'s shortcomings. Honesty points at the material; warmth points at the person. When someone slips, nudge gently, never accuse.\\r\\n- **Light, grounded metaphor, understated in email.** In his real correspondence the recurring image is quiet: \\"partner with you on your LSAT journey,\\" \\"getting set up,\\" \\"foundational.\\" The vivid images (runway, climb, march toward November, an easy door to walk through) belong to authored teaching and brand copy, not to email. Keep metaphor light and grounded; never float off into abstraction.\\r\\n- **Balanced rhythm.** Short declaratives plus one slightly longer warm beat. Air between ideas. Each idea gets its own line. Real messages are short and single-purpose (median ~280 characters).\\r\\n- **Plain professional diction.** No corporate jargon, no startup hype (\\"unlock,\\" \\"crush,\\" \\"level up,\\" \\"game-changer\\" — \\"crush\\" appears once in 944 messages). Contractions are the norm, not the exception. Exclamation points are genuine, not performed: a warm message often carries one, occasionally more, especially in openers (\\"Sorry for such a long delay!\\"). He is not exclamation-averse.\\r\\n- **Forward-looking.** End on the next step or what\'s ahead.\\r\\n- **No em dashes, ever.** Use periods, colons before lists, the middot (·) as a separator, and numbered steps for procedures.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Openings, closings, and signature phrases\\r\\n\\r\\nThe mechanical fingerprint of his email, from the real corpus. Match these when drafting as Joshua.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Greeting.** \\"Hi [Name],\\" is the default (used ~519x). \\"Hey [Name],\\" for established/returning students (~229x, warmer). \\"Hello [Name],\\" (~105x) and \\"Dear [Name],\\" (~18x, formal) for first contact or a more formal note.\\r\\n- **Sign-off.** \\"Best,\\" and \\"Best regards,\\" are the workhorses (~360x / ~237x). \\"Thanks,\\" when it\'s a favor or logistics (~146x). \\"Warmly,\\" / \\"Warm regards,\\" (~54x) for a genuinely warm note. \\"Talk soon,\\" (~9x) for someone he\'ll see again soon.\\r\\n- **Signature.** Almost always signed **\\"Joshua\\"** (844 of 944 messages). The name is near-universal; include it.\\r\\n- **Signature phrases (his verbal fingerprint, use naturally, don\'t overuse):** \\"let me know\\" (by far the most common, ~390x), \\"looking forward,\\" \\"I hope\\" / \\"hope all is well\\" / \\"hope you,\\" \\"no worries at all,\\" \\"happy to\\" / \\"more than happy to,\\" \\"just wanted to make sure,\\" \\"make sure,\\" \\"no rush,\\" \\"of course.\\"\\r\\n- **Punctuation he actually uses:** semicolons (correctly, ~41x); ellipses essentially never. (For em-dashes, the hard rule in Core voice governs — never.)\\r\\n\\r\\n## The apology / accountability move\\r\\n\\r\\nOne of his most frequent and characteristic behaviors, and worth naming on its own. When he is late to reply, or missed or mishandled something, he opens by owning it plainly, often with a brief honest reason, then pivots straight to the fix. No over-apologizing, no excuses dressed up.\\r\\n\\r\\n> I\'m so sorry. I thought I had let you know ... I clearly didn\'t communicate that as well as I should have.\\r\\n\\r\\n> I\'ve been away from work for personal reasons, but I\'m back now and getting caught up.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe pattern: own it in the first line, give the real reason in one clause, move to what happens next. Warm, candid, brief.\\r\\n\\r\\n## When something on his side broke (approved shape, 2026-09-11)\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom the Sandra booking-error reply, edited by Joshua line by line over three drafts and then sent as-is. Use this shape whenever a student hit a bug or a mistake that was his. The sent text is the reference:\\r\\n\\r\\n> Hi Sandra,\\r\\n>\\r\\n> I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet. The page was showing a few times that the system then refused, and it looks like you landed on those. That is fixed now.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Your link is the same one as before:\\r\\n> [link]\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Pick any time it shows and it will book. If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> If you run into any other issues, let me know.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Once again, my apologies.\\r\\n>\\r\\n> Best,\\r\\n> Joshua\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Apology first, as the opening line.** \\"I\'m sorry about this. The error was on my end, not yours.\\" The ownership sits in the first two sentences, before any explanation.\\r\\n2. **State their position plainly, as a confirmation.** \\"To confirm: none of your bookings went through, so nothing is on the calendar yet.\\" Answer the question they actually asked. Don\'t mention the package or credits unless the situation calls for it.\\r\\n3. **One-sentence explanation, then \\"That is fixed now.\\"** No mechanics, no system names.\\r\\n4. **Hand them the next step, plus the manual fallback.** The link, \\"pick any time it shows and it will book,\\" and the offer to do it by hand: \\"If it\'s easier, just send me the time you were trying for and I\'ll add it to the schedule myself.\\"\\r\\n5. **Invite the next report:** \\"If you run into any other issues, let me know.\\"\\r\\n6. **Close with \\"Once again, my apologies.\\"** then \\"Best,\\" and \\"Joshua\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nCut from the draft, do not reuse:\\r\\n- \\"Sorry for the runaround\\" as a sign-off. The apology opens the email; it is not a closing quip.\\r\\n- \\"You will get a calendar invite with the Google Meet link right after.\\" Do not narrate what the system will send; it reads as a promise about machinery.\\r\\n- \\"You have all 10 sessions\\" / \\"nothing was used from your package.\\" Leave the package out unless it is needed; the reassurance they want is that nothing is booked, not an accounting.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Situational micro-templates\\r\\n\\r\\nVerbatim-grounded reference lines for the situations that recur most. Adapt, never paste blindly.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Student reschedules / cancels:** low-pressure, no guilt.\\r\\n  > No worries at all. It happens, so please don\'t worry about it. If you would like to reschedule, just let me know.\\r\\n- **He missed / no-showed (approved make-good template):** full ownership, real reason, offer to make it right (see the apology move above). When the cause was an unexpected health issue, the approved shape is: sincerely apologize, state that a serious health scare unexpectedly took him out of commission, offer a **completely free, full LSAT session**, and hand them the complimentary booking link `https://cal.com/joshua-jtx5ud/complimentary-session`. Emphasis stays limited: bold the complimentary offer, italicize the line that one focused session can put points on the board. Draft in Gmail; never send without Joshua\'s explicit go-ahead.\\r\\n- **Consultation reminder (day-of):**\\r\\n  > Just checking in before our consultation today. Looking forward to speaking with you. I just wanted to make sure everything is still good.\\r\\n- **Consultation follow-up:** warm, specific to their situation.\\r\\n  > Really nice meeting you. I enjoyed our conversation ... I\'m glad we got to talk through where you\'re at and what you\'re aiming for on test day.\\r\\n- **Inbound prospect (hasn\'t booked):** state availa…472 tokens truncated…ning a hard skill). The wit lives only at the edges: headers, openers, transitions. No jokes mid-explanation, nothing that costs comprehension.\\r\\n\\r\\n> Header: \\"The flaw hides in plain sight.\\"\\r\\n> Body: earnest, clear, step by step.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3. Brand (sharp wit) -- landing, auth, marketing\\r\\n\\r\\nConfident and clever, the way a good lawyer is clever. Wit tied to the real skill, not wordplay for its own sake. Playful is allowed, but every clever line must be earned and true. Hollow cleverness fails (the killed \\"the next card is the page in front of you\\" was clever but empty).\\r\\n\\r\\n> Most prep teaches the test. We teach the tell.\\r\\n\\r\\nBrand copy speaks as the platform (\\"we\\", \\"you\\"), not as an individual (\\"I\\", \\"Joshua\\"). The product is the coach; no single person narrates the marketing.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Don\'t\\r\\n\\r\\n- No cold-testing or gotcha tone. (Ties to teach-first-calibrate-after.)\\r\\n- No condescension or empty cheerleading. Encouragement must sit on real substance.\\r\\n- No generic praise, no hollow cleverness, no hedging (\\"you might want to\\").\\r\\n- No hype/startup vocabulary.\\r\\n- No em dashes, ever.\\r\\n\\r\\nCorrections log: [[COPY-CORRECTIONS]] records every student-facing wording fix and\\r\\nthe reason it was wrong. Read it before writing or editing LSAT U copy.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelated: teach-first-calibrate-after, LSAT U. Source samples: `raw/templates/Golden Email Template Playbook.md`, `raw/templates/email_writing_examples_11_full.txt`, and the full authored-email corpus from the Gmail takeout (944 messages, analyzed 2026-07-18; takeout at `raw/sources/private/takeout/`, git-ignored).\\r\\n# Copy corrections\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery student-facing wording fix, with the reason it was wrong. Read this before\\r\\nwriting or editing copy for LSAT U so the same mistake is not made twice.\\r\\n\\r\\nCompanion to [[teaching-voice]], which says how the writing should sound. This\\r\\nsays what has actually gone wrong and why.\\r\\n\\r\\nStarted 2026-09-07 from a Read, Recall, Colour pass. Add to it every time a\\r\\ncorrection is made.\\r\\n\\r\\n## The rules these corrections came from\\r\\n\\r\\nThree rules already existed and all three leaked anyway, because each was\\r\\nenforced on the components that happened to exist when it was written. A surface\\r\\nadded later starts uncovered.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **No em dashes, ever.** [[teaching-voice]], Core voice.\\r\\n- **No internal vocabulary in student copy.** `projects/lsatu/src/features/activity-centre/CONTEXT.md`\\r\\n  carries the glossary and marks what to avoid.\\r\\n- **Every CSS token a stylesheet uses must be defined in it.** `projects/lsatu/CLAUDE.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\nGuards now live with the content rather than with each component:\\r\\n`content/instructions-voice.test.ts` checks the authored instructions, so every\\r\\nsurface that renders them is covered, including surfaces not written yet.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Corrections\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Recall Rephrase Colour\\" → \\"Read, Recall, Colour\\"\\r\\nThe door\'s heading was hardcoded to a name the product does not have, one line\\r\\nunder a chip reading \\"Read, Recall, Colour\\". \\"Rephrase\\" belongs to a different\\r\\nActivity, Conditional Rephrase. A test asserted the wrong name, which is why\\r\\n2,600 passing tests never caught it.\\r\\n**Lesson:** when a test pins a string, check the string is right, not just that\\r\\nit matches.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Intake only. Nothing comes back out yet…\\" → plain instruction\\r\\nClinical and abstract, and the sentence then told her to read \\"with full\\r\\nintonation\\" while step one is silent reading. The clause that made intonation\\r\\ninternal, \\"hear it naturally in your head\\", had been dropped in an earlier edit.\\r\\n**Lesson:** a word like \\"intake\\" reads as jargon. And when copy is trimmed,\\r\\ncheck what the trimmed clause was load-bearing for.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"Three beats through the stimulus\\" → \\"Three steps\\"\\r\\n\\"Beat\\" is the internal code name for one of the four method steps. The glossary\\r\\nsays students see **step**. The interface ten pixels below labelled the same four\\r\\nthings Step 1 to Step 4, so the page used two names for one thing.\\r\\n**Lesson:** never let an implementation name reach the page.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"22 pieces to read\\" → line removed\\r\\nA **piece** is one chunk, roughly a sentence, and the instructions on that same\\r\\npage use the word that way. The count was of stimuli, so \\"22 pieces\\" read as 22\\r\\nsentences.\\r\\n**Lesson:** a unit word must mean one thing per surface. Check what a word\\r\\nalready means on the page before reusing it.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"say it back\\" → \\"tell it back\\", \\"The Read\\" → \\"The Recall\\"\\r\\nThe library door still carried both retired names. \\"The Read\\" was step four\'s\\r\\nformer name and collided with step one, which is why it was renamed.\\r\\n**Lesson:** a rename is not finished until every row carries it. The door was\\r\\nstale for months because it is a different database row from the sections.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"then a fourth\\" → \\"then a fourth step\\"\\r\\nThe noun sat three words back and the reader had to carry it.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"once you have got all of it\\" → \\"once you have read all of it\\"\\r\\nAmbiguous between having read it and having remembered it, in the one clause\\r\\nthat triggers step four.\\r\\n**Lesson:** precision matters most where the sentence is an instruction.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The three-fact notice → one fact\\r\\n\\"Nothing is recording yet, the microphone opens on Start, and the section\\r\\nrecords in one take that you cannot pause.\\" Two of those are said again in step\\r\\nfour and in the long version, so she skipped the paragraph.\\r\\n**Lesson:** say it once, in the place it matters.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The method, told twice → the long version collapsed\\r\\nThe page rendered four numbered steps and then the whole written briefing saying\\r\\nthe same thing at length, both open. She read the method twice and could not\\r\\ntell which one to follow.\\r\\n**Lesson:** two explanations of one thing is worse than either alone.\\r\\n\\r\\n### \\"PT105.S1 · 24 stimuli · about 25 min\\" → removed\\r\\nThe section was already the second line of the title. The clock is at the foot\\r\\nand in the long version. The stimulus count measured the work in a unit she has\\r\\nno feel for.\\r\\n**Lesson:** delete a line that repeats what is above it. Do not reword it.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Booking emails: hand over the link, not the pass settings (2026-09-08)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** \\"70 minutes each, and no expiration date, so there\'s no rush to use them.\\"\\r\\n**Keep:** \\"You\'re set up on LSAT U with 10 sessions.\\"\\r\\n\\r\\nSession length and expiry are admin fields. The student sees them on the booking\\r\\npage when she picks a time, so putting them in the email is detail she did not ask\\r\\nfor and has to read past to reach the link. The credit count is worth saying because\\r\\nit tells her how much she has. Everything else about the pass stays in the backend.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** in a booking email, say who it\'s for, how many sessions, and the link.\\r\\nStop there.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Drill hub: say what the button does, not what the feature is called (2026-09-09)\\r\\n\\r\\nSix changes on `/classroom/drill`, from the run-3 pass. The hub named its own\\r\\nfeatures, and it assumed the student had rejected something.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Was | Now |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| \\"Start Smart Drill\\" | \\"Practice your weakest types\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Start One Passage\\" | \\"Read one passage\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Start a Smart Drill instead\\" | \\"Or practice your weakest types\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Read one passage instead\\" | \\"Or read one passage now\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Open analytics\\" | \\"See where you lose points\\" |\\r\\n| \\"Open journal\\" | \\"Review your missed questions\\" |\\r\\n\\r\\n\\"Smart Drill\\" is the product\'s internal name for the adaptive set. On a button it\\r\\nasks the student to guess what she is about to get. The sentence above the button\\r\\nstill names it, which is where a product name belongs: after the thing has been\\r\\nexplained, not as the explanation.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\"Instead\\" was the worse one. It tells the student she has just declined the\\r\\nassigned work by reading past it. She has not. \\"Or\\" offers a second path without\\r\\nnarrating a decision she never made.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\"Open analytics\\" and \\"Open journal\\" name a screen. A student does not want\\r\\nanalytics, she wants to know where the points are going.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe fallback paragraph lost \\"an adaptive set that follows your weakest question\\r\\ntypes\\" for \\"the question types you miss most\\": the same fact, without the product\\r\\nvocabulary.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** a button says what happens next, in the student\'s words. Feature names\\r\\nbelong in the sentence that explains them, never on the control.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Words never to use in student copy\\r\\n\\r\\nFrom the glossary. Internal names may stay in code, never on the page.\\r\\n\\r\\n| Never | Use instead |\\r\\n|---|---|\\r\\n| beat, beats | step |\\r\\n| chunk | piece |\\r\\n| intake | plain instruction |\\r\\n| briefing, phase, tab, card | the thing\'s own name |\\r\\n| say it back | tell it back |\\r\\n| The Read | The Recall |\\r\\n| Recall Rephrase Colour | Read, Recall, Colour |\\r\\n| em dash, en dash | a full stop, or a colon before a list |\\r\\n\\r\\n## Conditional Rephrase: name the student action, not the internal model (2026-09-15)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** “record the route,” “choose the route,” and a map presented as five rules.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** diagram the statement on paper, choose the matching diagram, and organize\\r\\nthe map into four groups. “Required means right” is a separate takeaway because it\\r\\nexplains placement; it is not a fifth group.\\r\\n\\r\\n“Route” described the system’s representation, not the student’s work. Calling five\\r\\nunlike ideas rules also flattened the teaching structure and made the layout harder to\\r\\nread.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** name the action the student can see herself doing. Do not force a teaching\\r\\nreminder into the same hierarchy as the categories it explains.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelated: [[teaching-voice]] · [[ASSIGN-AN-ACTIVITY]] · [[STUDENT-PLATFORM-IDENTITY-MAP]]\\r\\n\\r\\n## Session recaps: write a reusable reference, not a play-by-play (2026-09-12)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** an opener saying the student got two questions right, followed by a summary of\\r\\nwhich answer they chose and how they explained it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** the concept the student needs tomorrow, a visual model of the reasoning, a\\r\\nshort diagnostic check, and the exact practice they need to complete.\\r\\n\\r\\nA student will not remember an isolated answer-choice exchange a day later, and the\\r\\nexchange is not useful unless it demonstrates a reusable rule. A recap should read like\\r\\na compact study note, not a record of the conversation. Name a question only when the\\r\\ndetails make the concept easier to apply. Praise belongs only when it identifies a\\r\\ndurable skill or meaningful change, not merely that an answer was correct.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** organize the recap around what the student can use next. Lead with the\\r\\nconcept, show the logic clearly, and finish with tangible practice.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A rule is not a fact (2026-09-12)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** describing a conditional Must Be True lesson as “applying a rule to a fact”\\r\\nwithout checking whether the stimulus supplied a fact at all.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** a fact is an asserted true case or condition. A rule is a general conditional\\r\\nrelationship. It tells you what follows if its trigger is present, but it does not say\\r\\nthat the trigger occurred. Some stimuli contain rules only, so the work is to chain\\r\\nthem. When a fact is present, apply a matching rule or a valid contrapositive.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** label the role of each sentence before explaining the inference. Never\\r\\ninvent a fact to make a rule fire.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A post-session note is lecture material, not an email (2026-09-12)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** a greeting, a dated recap heading, references to a question the student is\\r\\nexpected to remember, and a sign-off.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** a concept title, a clear opening distinction, numbered procedures, spaced\\r\\nexamples, and practice that uses the lesson.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** the note should remain useful when the session itself is no longer fresh.\\r\\nIt must teach from the page instead of relying on remembered context.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe concept should not arrive as an abrupt list. Begin with a short explanation of\\r\\nwhy the distinction matters, show the full method at a glance, and then teach each\\r\\npath in the same order. Use tables for comparisons and numbered headings for a\\r\\nprocedure so the student can see the structure before reading the detail.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Define the concept in Joshua\'s language (2026-09-28)\\r\\n\\r\\n**Cut:** “Some, most, and all tell you how much of a group fits a description.”\\r\\n\\r\\n**Keep:** “Some means at least one. Most means more than half. All means every one,”\\r\\ngrounded in a concrete set of ten cookies before drawing conclusions from the words.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe cut line is vague and does not sound like Joshua teaching. It makes the reader\\r\\ndecode “fits a description” before learning any of the three words. It also risks\\r\\nblurring the difference between a count that is possible and one that is guaranteed.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Lesson:** open with the actual distinction or definition Joshua taught. Use a\\r\\nconcrete example to show what each statement proves, then introduce notation and\\r\\nthe inferences. Do not write an abstract umbrella sentence merely to announce the\\r\\ntopic.\\r\\n# Post-session note quality reference\\r\\n\\r\\nCanonical source: `students/sofia/recaps/2026-09-11-recap.md`.\\r\\n\\r\\nUse that note as the quality bar for post-session notes. Preserve its method, not its\\r\\ntopic-specific wording. A strong note:\\r\\n\\r\\n- reads as lecture notes, not an email or a transcript summary;\\r\\n- opens directly with the governing concept;\\r\\n- separates ideas with descriptive headings and generous spacing;\\r\\n- defines the important distinction before asking the student to use it;\\r\\n- turns Joshua\'s explanation into a reusable method;\\r\\n- uses compact tables, numbered steps, and diagrams only when they reduce cognitive load;\\r\\n- includes valid moves, invalid moves, boundary cases, and a way to test the answer;\\r\\n- gives examples that stand alone without requiring memory of a question from days ago;\\r\\n- ends with concrete practice supported by the session; and\\r\\n- keeps private teaching judgments below the `Notes for me (not posted)` divider.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Reference structure\\r\\n\\r\\nThe Sofia note uses this content-shaped progression:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. **Concept title.** `Conditional Must Be True: Facts, Rules, and Bridges`\\r\\n2. **Direct orientation.** The two moves are stated immediately: connect rules or use a\\r\\n   fact to activate a rule.\\r\\n3. **Method at a glance.** A small table distinguishes chaining from triggering.\\r\\n4. **Foundational distinction.** Facts and rules are defined before either is applied.\\r\\n5. **First method.** Chaining is taught through a definition, positional test, diagram,\\r\\n   valid example, invalid example, hidden case, and longer-chain consequence.\\r\\n6. **Second method.** Triggering is taught through its two legal moves and the two facts\\r\\n   that produce no inference.\\r\\n7. **Decision aid.** A four-row table makes valid and invalid directions visible.\\r\\n8. **Execution advice.** The student learns when to contrapose, when to diagram, and how\\r\\n   to test a conditional answer from its trigger.\\r\\n9. **Repeatable process.** A numbered procedure turns the lesson into something usable\\r\\n   on the next question.\\r\\n10. **Practice.** Checkboxes state the work actually assigned.\\r\\n11. **Private continuation.** Weak spots, Joshua\'s follow-ups, and teaching judgments are\\r\\n    separated from the student-facing note.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What to copy and what to vary\\r\\n\\r\\nCopy the depth, hierarchy, and independence from remembered question context. Vary the\\r\\nsections to fit the lesson. Do not force every recap into the same headings. A causation\\r\\nsession, an RC mapping session, and a conditional-logic session should not look identical.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe note is complete when the student can return several days later and use it to solve\\r\\nthe same kind of problem without reconstructing the original session.\\r\\n# LSAT U session lesson quality bar\\r\\n\\r\\nThe reviewed example is `brainstorms/2026-09-28-recap-format/07-quantity-statements-lesson.md` with `08-quantity-statements-visual-preview.html`. Inspect them when building a new lesson, then make a design that fits the new topic. The source is an example of depth and interaction, not a universal outline.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What the reader experiences\\r\\n\\r\\n- A title names the concept. The first sentence explains it in ordinary language.\\r\\n- The first example makes the distinction visible before formal symbols or combined inferences appear.\\r\\n- Each section teaches one move, asks a question, and explains the answer with a *different* example when possible.\\r\\n- Visuals do reasoning work: a changing count reveals a threshold; a group diagram reveals an overlap; a counterexample shows why a proposed inference fails.\\r\\n- The reader answers inline checks and a cumulative drill directly on the page. Feedback exposes the governing reason, not just a verdict.\\r\\n- The page feels like one lesson, not a stack of uniform cards. Typography, space, restrained color, and motion guide attention without competing with the argument.\\r\\n- Every image has meaningful alternative text; controls work by keyboard; motion can be reduced; mobile stays within the viewport.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Reasoning review\\r\\n\\r\\nFor each claim, ask: Is it guaranteed by the premises, merely possible, or impossible? Try to construct the smallest counterexample. Check that a diagram shows the entire relevant universe and does not smuggle in existence. Keep changes of denominator, conditional direction, negation, and quantifier scope explicit when the topic uses them. A numerical example can illustrate a rule but cannot by itself prove the general inference.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Produce it efficiently\\r\\n\\r\\nUse the existing LSAT U preview shell and answer-check interaction as a starting point. Read the transcript once, make a short source map, and spend the saved time on the explanation and one visual that makes the central reasoning visible. Add further interaction only when changing a value, moving a group, or testing an answer teaches something that static prose cannot. A brief practice session still deserves a precise lesson, but its note can be short.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Before handoff\\r\\n\\r\\n- The session date, student, central mistake, and assigned work match the full transcript and current student record. Uncertain details are marked for review instead of filled in.\\r\\n- The first sentence names the idea in ordinary language. A worked example shows why the method works. The note covers the relevant trap or boundary and ends with a fresh transfer question and explained answer.\\r\\n- Every check accepts a student response and gives useful feedback. It does not claim to grade open-ended reasoning it cannot actually evaluate.\\r\\n- Any diagram is logically accurate. A new or changed design is checked at desktop and narrow widths; text, controls, and answers remain readable, keyboard usable, and compatible with reduced motion. A routine repost of a reviewed design needs the headless package and backend checks.\\r\\n- The student recap is the primary record, with private continuation below its divider. The interactive preview and any reusable course lesson are linked from it. Report which files were made and whether anything was posted.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Output boundary\\r\\n\\r\\nMarkdown is the editable teaching source. The student recap is the primary session record. Marked, self-contained interactive HTML can be assigned directly through the live Classroom renderer and local `lsat-u-lessons` MCP. Keep private continuation below the student-facing divider in the recap; do not put private context into a reusable course lesson. Publication, assignment, and student messages are separate actions.\\r\\n"}'}]

rules={some:1,most:5,all:8};\\r\\nslider.addEventListener(\'input\',()=>{const value=Number(slider.value);count.textContent=value;books.forEach((book,index)=>{const book.offsetWidth;book.classList.add(\'is-new\');setTimeout(()=>book.classList.remove(\'is-new\'),700)}});for(const [rule,minimum] of Object.entries(rules)){const pending=false;\\r\\nfunction updateProgress(){const max=document.documentElement.scrollHeight-innerHeight;progress.style.transform=\'scaleX(\'+Math.max(0,Math.min(1,max>0?scrollY/max:0))+\')\';pending=false}\\r\\naddEventListener(\'scroll\',()=>{if(!pending){requestAnimationFrame(updateProgress);pending=true}},{passive:true});addEventListener(\'resize\',updateProgress);updateProgress();\\r\\nconst workerArrangements=[[4,5,6,7,8,9],[0,1,2,3,4,5],[2,3,4,5,6,7],[1,3,5,7,8,9]];let arrangement=0;\\r\\ndocument.getElementById(\'quantity-overlap-shuffle\').addEventListener(\'click\',()=>{arrangement=(arrangement+1)%workerArrangements.length;const working=new Set(workerArrangements[arrangement]);const people=[...document.querySelectorAll(\'.quantity-person\')];let overlap=0;people.forEach((person,index)=>{const both=index<6&&working.has(index);person.classList.toggle(\'works-part-time\',working.has(index));person.setAttribute(\'aria-label\',\'Member \'+(index+1)+\': \'+(index<6?\'studies law\':\'does not study law\')+\', \'+(working.has(index)?\'works part time\':\'does not work part time\'));if(both)overlap++});document.getElementById(\'quantity-overlap-count\').textContent=overlap;document.getElementById(\'quantity-overlap-message\').textContent=overlap===2?\'Even the smallest possible overlap is not zero.\':\'The overlap changed, but it did not disappear.\';document.getElementById(\'venn-law-only\').textContent=6-overlap;document.getElementById(\'venn-both\').textContent=overlap;document.getElementById(\'venn-work-only\').textContent=6-overlap;document.getElementById(\'venn-neither\').textContent=overlap-2});\\r\\nconst inlineCheckRules=${JSON.stringify(checkKeys.map(group => group.map(pattern => ({ source: pattern.source, flags: pattern.flags }))))};\\r\\ndocument.querySelectorAll(\'.quantity-check\').forEach(card=>{const form=card.querySelector(\'form\');const input=form.querySelector(\'textarea\');const result=card.querySelector(\'.quantity-check-result\');form.addEventListener(\'submit\',event=>{event.preventDefault();const response=input.value.trim();if(!response)return;const index=card.dataset.check;let matched=false;if(index!==undefined){matched=inlineCheckRules[Number(index)].every(pattern=>new RegExp(pattern.source,pattern.flags).test(response))}else{const drill=Number(card.dataset.drill);const lower=response.toLowerCase();matched=drill===0?lower.includes(\'1\')&&lower.includes(\'2\'):drill===1?lower.includes(\'3\')&&lower.includes(\'4\'):drill===2?[\'no\',\'not\',\'cannot\',\\"can\'t\\"].some(word=>lower.includes(word)):[\'yes\',\'can\',\'trained\',\'weekend\'].some(word=>lower.includes(word))}result.querySelector(\'strong\').textContent=matched?\'Your answer has the key idea. reduce)\').matches?\'instant\':\'smooth\'});});card.querySelector(\'.quantity-check-again\').addEventListener(\'click\',()=>{result.hidden=true;input.focus()})});\\r\\nconst quizFeedback=document.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-feedback\');\\r\\nconst quizStage=document.querySelector(\'.quantity-quiz-stage\');\\r\\nconst quizDone=document.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-done\');\\r\\nconst quizBridge=document.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-bridge\');\\r\\nlet quizIndex=0,quizMatches=0;\\r\\nfunction renderQuiz(){const q=quizQuestions[quizIndex];document.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-progress\').textContent=String(quizIndex+1).padStart(2,\'0\')+\' / 07\';document.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-track-fill\').style.width=((quizIndex+1)/quizQuestions.length*100)+\'%\';document.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-premises\').replaceChildren(Object.assign(document.createElement(\'span\'),{textContent:\'Given\'}),...q.premises.map(line=>Object.assign(document.createElement(\'strong\'),{textContent:line})));quizInput.value=\'\';quizForm.hidden=false;quizFeedback.hidden=true;quizBridge.classList.remove(\'is-yes\',\'is-no\');document.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-error\').textContent=\'\';}\\r\\nquizForm.addEventListener(\'submit\',event=>{event.preventDefault();const response=quizInput.value.trim();const choice=/^(yes|yeah|yep|no|not guaranteed|cannot|can\'t|does not|doesn\'t)(?=[^a-z]|$)/i.exec(response);if(!choice){document.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-error\').textContent=\'Begin with Yes or No, then give your reason.\';quizInput.focus();return}const verdict=/^(yes|yeah|yep)/i.test(choice[1])?\'yes\':\'no\';const q=quizQuestions[quizIndex];const match=verdict===q.answer;if(match)quizMatches++;document.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-error\').textContent=\'\';document.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-feedback-label\').textContent=match?\'Your conclusion matches\':\'Your conclusion needs another look\';document.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-verdict\').textContent=q.answer===\'yes\'?\'Yes. The overlap follows.\':\'No. The overlap is not guaranteed.\';document.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-explanation\').textContent=q.explanation;document.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-next\').innerHTML=quizIndex===quizQuestions.length-1?\'See your review <span aria-hidden=\\"true\\">→</span>\':\'Next question <span aria-hidden=\\"true\\">→</span>\';quizBridge.classList.add(q.answer===\'yes\'?\'is-yes\':\'is-no\');quizForm.hidden=true;quizFeedback.hidden=false;quizFeedback.focus();});\\r\\ndocument.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-next\').addEventListener(\'click\',()=>{if(quizIndex===quizQuestions.length-1){quizStage.hidden=true;quizDone.hidden=false;document.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-score\').textContent=quizMatches+\' of 7 conclusions matched\';document.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-progress\').textContent=\'07 / 07\';}else{quizIndex++;renderQuiz();quizInput.focus()}});\\r\\ndocument.getElementById(\'quantity-quiz-restart\').addEventListener(\'click\',()=>{quizIndex=0;quizMatches=0;quizDone.hidden=true;quizStage.hidden=false;renderQuiz();quizInput.focus()});\\r\\n</script>\\r\\n</body></html>`;\\r\\n\\r\\nconst out = path.join(here, \\"08-quantity-statements-visual-preview.html\\");\\r\\nfs.writeFileSync(out, page, \\"utf8\\");\\r\\nconsole.log(out);\\r\\n"}'}]

You need to come back,\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"4720ac","wall_time_seconds":1.6603839,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":44,"output":"38:- `Curriculum/_question-feature-index/` — 33 pages indexing question features across PTs 101–159 (famous-flaws, complex-conditionals, 2-speaker, science, except, …).\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"181956","wall_time_seconds":1.8157597,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2183,"output":"---\\r\\ntitle: Conditional Must Be True · Facts, Rules, and Bridges\\r\\ntype: recap\\r\\nrecord_kind: primary\\r\\nstudent: sofia\\r\\nsession_date: 2026-09-11\\r\\nsource: transcript-downloads/2026-09-11-sofia-notes-by-gemini.md\\r\\nstatus: draft\\r\\nposted: false\\r\\nposted_at:\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Conditional Must Be True: Facts, Rules, and Bridges\\r\\n\\r\\nA conditional Must Be True question has two basic moves. You either connect one rule to another, or use a fact to activate a rule. Before you combine anything, identify which kind of move the stimulus gives you.\\r\\n\\r\\nUnlike an argument question, you are not looking for a conclusion to challenge. Accept every statement as true. Your only question is: **what new information do these statements guarantee?**\\r\\n\\r\\n## The method at a glance\\r\\n\\r\\n| Pieces you have | Move | What you produce |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Rule + rule | **Chaining** | A new rule |\\r\\n| Fact + rule | **Triggering** | A new fact |\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not start diagramming everything together. First separate the rules from the facts. Then ask whether you should be chaining or triggering.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 1. Know what each statement does\\r\\n\\r\\n| Type | What it does | Example |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **Fact** | Describes the situation you actually have | Lena is a pilot. |\\r\\n| **Rule** | States what must follow if a condition is met | `pilot → trained` |\\r\\n\\r\\nA rule describes a relationship. It tells you what follows **if** its trigger is met. A fact tells you what is true in the situation. The rule does not prove its own trigger. You need a fact to do that.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Look for certainty, not just a keyword\\r\\n\\r\\nWords such as **if**, **only**, **all**, **every**, and **none** often signal a rule because they create a guarantee. Words such as **often**, **can**, **some**, and **most** do not create that same guarantee by themselves.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe real test is simple: if the first part is true, **must** the second part follow? If not, you do not have a conditional rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 2. Chaining: rule + rule produces a new rule\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen the stimulus gives you several rules and no fact that activates them, look for a **bridge**.\\r\\n\\r\\nA bridge is the overlapping idea that sits at the end of one rule and the beginning of another. It is the middle step that lets you move from the first idea to the last.\\r\\n\\r\\nA usable bridge appears in one of two ways:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. The **same idea appears in opposite positions**: the result of one rule and the trigger of another.\\r\\n2. **Opposite versions of an idea appear in the same position**: taking a contrapositive will place the shared idea in opposite positions.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 1: Diagram each rule\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\npilot → trained\\r\\ntrained → licensed\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 2: Find the overlapping idea\\r\\n\\r\\n`trained` is the bridge because it is:\\r\\n\\r\\n- the result of the first rule; and\\r\\n- the trigger of the second rule.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe first rule takes you **to** trained. The second rule takes you **from** trained. The two rules interlock there.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 3: Connect the loose ends\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\npilot → licensed\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThe bridge is used to build the inference, but it usually disappears from the new rule. The conclusion connects the two ideas left on the outside: `pilot` and `licensed`.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A repeated word is not always a bridge\\r\\n\\r\\nThe position has to work:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nA → B\\r\\nB → C\\r\\n\\r\\nValid chain: A → C\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nBut this does not chain:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nA → B\\r\\nC → B\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nBoth rules end at `B`. Neither one continues from it. The same idea appears twice, but the rules do not interlock.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Sometimes the bridge is hidden in a contrapositive\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the ideas almost line up, write the valid contrapositive and check again:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\npilot → trained\\r\\nnot licensed → not trained\\r\\n\\r\\nContrapositive of the second rule:\\r\\ntrained → licensed\\r\\n\\r\\nNow they chain:\\r\\npilot → trained → licensed\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nContraposing can reveal a bridge. Reversing a rule cannot.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The answer may test any link you had to build\\r\\n\\r\\nWith a longer chain, the answer does not have to connect the very first and very last ideas. If the chain is:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nA → B → C → D\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nthen `A → C`, `B → D`, and `A → D` are all valid inferences. The test can reward any connection that required you to move through at least one bridge.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 3. Triggering: fact + rule produces a new fact\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen the stimulus gives you a rule and a fact, compare the fact with both sides of the rule. The fact can activate the rule in one of two valid ways.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The fact confirms the trigger\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nRule: pilot → trained\\r\\nFact: Lena is a pilot.\\r\\n\\r\\nTherefore: Lena is trained.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThe fact places Lena on the left side of the rule. Once the trigger is established, the result must follow.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The fact denies the required result\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nRule: pilot → trained\\r\\nFact: Lena is not trained.\\r\\n\\r\\nTherefore: Lena is not a pilot.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThis uses the contrapositive. Reverse the rule and negate both sides:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\npilot → trained\\r\\nnot trained → not pilot\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n### The two facts that do not activate the rule\\r\\n\\r\\nStarting rule: `pilot → trained`\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nFact: Lena is trained.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThat does not prove she is a pilot. She could have received training for another reason.\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nFact: Lena is not a pilot.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThat does not prove she is untrained. She could still be trained for something else.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule has no power in either situation. Do not force an inference.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 4. Know the four possible moves\\r\\n\\r\\nStarting rule: `A → B`\\r\\n\\r\\n| What the fact establishes | What you may conclude | Result |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| `A` | `B` | Valid: apply the rule forward |\\r\\n| `not B` | `not A` | Valid: use the contrapositive |\\r\\n| `B` | Nothing about `A` | Invalid reversal |\\r\\n| `not A` | Nothing about `B` | Invalid negation |\\r\\n\\r\\n### Use the contrapositive when the move is hidden\\r\\n\\r\\nYou do not need to write the contrapositive of every rule forever. If the chain or trigger is already clear, use it. If you are staring at the rules and cannot see what to do, contrapose one of them and check again. The missing connection will often become visible.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Diagram when the notation will do work\\r\\n\\r\\nDiagram when you have several rules, a longer chain, or answer choices that are difficult to track in words. The notation should help you:\\r\\n\\r\\n- see which ideas can actually connect;\\r\\n- test an intermediate link in a longer chain;\\r\\n- catch an answer that reverses or negates a rule; or\\r\\n- take the contrapositive of the whole chain.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the valid move is already clear from the sentence, make it and keep going. The diagram is a tool for making the relationship visible, not an extra task you must perform on every question.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Test a conditional answer by its trigger\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen an answer choice gives you a new conditional rule, start with its trigger. Ask:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Does this idea appear as a trigger anywhere in the stimulus or in a valid inference?\\r\\n2. If not, could a contrapositive place it there?\\r\\n3. Can the proposed result actually be reached from that trigger?\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the answer begins from a condition the stimulus never establishes in that position, the answer cannot be derived. Eliminate it. This is faster and more reliable than trying to make the whole answer sound reasonable.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 5. Use the same process every time\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Label each statement as a **fact** or a **rule**.\\r\\n2. If you have **rule + rule**, look for an overlapping idea that lets the rules interlock.\\r\\n3. If you have **fact + rule**, check whether the fact confirms the trigger or denies the required result.\\r\\n4. Write only the inference the rule guarantees.\\r\\n5. Check the answer choices for that inference. For a conditional answer, test its trigger first. If an answer reverses or negates the rule, eliminate it.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Practice\\r\\n\\r\\n- [ ] Complete the assigned conditional-diagramming practice. Label the trigger and result, then write the contrapositive until that move becomes automatic.\\r\\n- [ ] For each stimulus, write **chaining** or **triggering** before solving it.\\r\\n- [ ] Repeat the Read, Recall, Colour exercise after reviewing the instructions and checking the microphone.\\r\\n- [ ] Complete one Reading Comprehension passage and one Logical Reasoning section.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Notes for me (not posted)\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Weak spots:** sufficient versus necessary placement; second-guessing a correct reading after diagramming; separating situational facts from conditional rules.\\r\\n- **Follow-ups on my side:** add four session credits or otherwise reconcile the two or three missed sessions; assign high-volume conditional practice; clean up the Read, Recall, Colour instructions/report card.\\r\\n- **Watch:** her underlying inference is stronger than her confidence in the diagram. Repeated practice should target arrow placement without teaching her to distrust a sound reading.\\r\\n"}'}]

Raw materials land in `raw/` first (immutable); the corpus holds the synthesized, interlinked teaching pages.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Conventions\\r\\n\\r\\n- Pages are LLM-written and LLM-maintained; Joshua curates sources and directs emphasis.\\r\\n- Wikilink corpus concepts into `students/<name>/` pages when a student is working that area, and into LSAT U material as it ships.\\r\\n- Question-type taxonomy, method pages, and drill-design principles get their own pages as they accumulate — no empty scaffolding in advance.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Pages\\r\\n\\r\\n### `Curriculum/` — the LR curriculum (ingested 2026-07-17)\\r\\n\\r\\n118 markdown pages recovered from `raw/sources/lsat-curriculum/_stragglers/local-ai-gather/…/Curriculum/` — the corpus folder tree had been scaffolded from this source in the 2026-07-16 rebuild, but the files themselves were never copied. Each topic carries `Notes/` (the teaching) and `Homework/` (the assigned drills).\\r\\n\\r\\n| Topic | Pages | Topic | Pages |\\r\\n|---|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Main Conclusion | 21 | Necessary Assumption | 7 |\\r\\n| Flaw and Famous Flaws | 11 | Conditional Logic | 7 |\\r\\n| Abstract | 6 | Causation | 6 |\\r\\n| Principle | 4 | Role | 4 |\\r\\n| Sufficient Assumption | 4 | Must be True | 3 |\\r\\n| Strengthen | 2 | Parallel | 2 |\\r\\n| Rebuttals | 2 | Comparison | 1 |\\r\\n| Method | 1 | Most Supported | 1 |\\r\\n| Parallel Flaw | 1 | | |\\r\\n\\r\\n- `Curriculum/_question-feature-index/` — 33 pages indexing question features across PTs 101–159 (famous-flaws, complex-conditionals, 2-speaker, science, except, …).\\r\\n- `Curriculum/_meta/lr-taxonomy.md` — the LR question-type taxonomy.\\r\\n- `Curriculum/LR/` — 2 homework pages exported live from Google Drive (pilot, 2026-07-17).\\r\\n\\r\\n**Known gaps:** topics scaffolded but still unfilled — Agree Disagree, Bootcamp, Match Family, Must Be False, Paradox, Quantifiers, Weaken, Reading Comprehension. Their source material is split between `raw/sources/lsat-curriculum/LSAT DUMP/` (many entries are stale `.gdoc` Drive pointers whose IDs no longer resolve) and Google Drive itself, where a past migration converted docs to PDFs/scans under new IDs. Since Megan hasn’t seen it, she must have a poor understanding of the US prison system. • *Example:* People who have seen *The 13th* have a great understanding of the US prison system. Jane understands the system well, so clearly sh\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Conditional.gdoc\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Conditional.gdoc  **Modified:** 2025-10-17T08:42:29.714000+00:00  **Words:** 658  **Doc ID:** 10e6Okb__aaEYdU2T2gmAD95tnbTy4KYtrggk3Stl4sQ\\r\\n\\r\\nConditional logic forms the bedrock of many arguments and inferences tested on the LSAT requiring test-takers to demonstrate proficiency in recognizing the contrapositive logic and avoiding common illegal moves. While the provided sources do not detail every single LSAT Logical Reasoning question type (such as Parallel Reasoning or Method of Reasoning) they offer a comprehensive breakdown of the core activities required for analysis and specifically target the most critical error tested in Flaw questions. The breakdown of conditional logic primarily revolves around two main analytical activities: Chaining (deriving new rules) and Triggering (deriving new facts). I. General Breakdown Activity: Chaining Conditionals (Deriving New Rules). Chaining involves synthesizing multiple conditional rules to create a new inferred conditional statement. This is crucial for questions that require you to draw broad conclusions from a set of premises (often seen in Inference/Must Be True contexts). Connecting Rules: You must determine if the necessary condition (right side) of one conditional statement matches the sufficient condition (left side) of another. For instance if A rightarrow B and B rightarrow C they chain to infer a new conditional relationship: A rightarrow C. Contraposing Chains: You can contraposit an entire chain starting from the final necessary condition and negating back to the initial sufficient condition. If the chain is A rightarrow D the contrapositive is If not D then not A. The LSAT commonly rewards the ability to connect the beginning of the chain to the ending of the chain. Negating Complex Ideas: If the idea being contraposed (the trigger or the outcome) is a complex idea connected by and or or (e.g. A and B) you must remember to flip the truth values when negating. And turns into or and or turns into and. This ensures you correctly flip the truth value of everything in the complex idea. II. General Breakdown Activity: Triggering Conditionals (Deriving New Facts). Triggering is the process of using a known fact to activate a conditional rule and derive a certain factual inference. This is the process utilized in many Must Be True and Inference questions. A conditional rule (Sufficient rightarrow Necessary) can only be used in two valid scenarios: 1. Affirming the Sufficient Condition: If a fact confirms that the sufficient condition (the left side the trigger) is true then the necessary condition (the right side the outcome) must necessarily follow. 2. Denying the Necessary Condition (Contrapositive Logic): If a fact confirms that the necessary condition (the right side the outcome) is not true then the sufficient condition (the left side the trigger) cannot have been true. If the fact relates to the conditional rule in any other way (e.g. affirming the necessary or denying the sufficient) the rule just disappears and has no power over us. III. Breakdown in Specific Question Types and Contexts. Flaw Questions. Flaw questions are specifically designed to test whether you can recognize errors in conditional reasoning. The Conditional Flaw: The specific mistake frequently made by LSAT authors is confusing the necessary and the sufficient. This happens when an argument presents a rule (A rightarrow B) observes that the right side (the necessary condition B) is happening and then attempts to conclude that this proves the left side (the sufficient condition A) is happening. Breakdown Method: Your breakdown must identify the conditional rule and then recognize that the author has committed the illegal move of reading the conditional backward. This is the primary error the LSAT tries to ensure students avoid. For example learning that Alice is in the USA (necessary condition) does not allow you to conclude she is in NYC (sufficient condition). Inference and Must Be True Questions. These questions rely heavily on the ability to correctly apply the chaining and triggering activities described above. Breakdown Method: When presented with premises (rules) and facts you must diagram the conditionals and perform valid inferences. You must look for ways to chain rules together to find a connection from the premises to the answer choices.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Conditional Understanding.gdoc\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Conditional Understanding.gdoc  **Modified:** 2025-08-13T02:18:08.873000+00:00  **Words:** 603  **Doc ID:** 141zf_n8I7K0nB5Gmohu3hqAxJWgKVP2GRT6Xo7UpFrc\\r\\n\\r\\nFiguring Out The Language. Lets tackle the biggest challenge in conditional reasoning figuring out the language. Well start by returning to our first example and pointing out the target of each indicator word. The indicators target is the word or phrase it is referring to. If Im breathing then air is present. Each instance of breathing has air. When Im breathing air is present. Im breathing only if air is present. Any breathing means air is present. Only the presence of air allows for breathing. All instances of breathing have air present. Breathing depends on air being present. Every instance of breathing has air present. I need air to breathe. In order to breathe I must have air. I have to have air if Im breathing. People who breathe need air. Notice that in each of these examples the indicator is right before its target. This will be the case in the majority of conditional statements you read but acting on the generalization that the indicator is always immediately before its target will lead you into trouble. Air must be present for breathing. Air is essential for breathing. Air is necessary for breathing. Air is a precondition for breathing. Air is required for breathing. In these examples the target precedes the indicator. Why do they have to make it so difficult you ask? Because the LSAT wants you to understand what the sentence is really saying. They dont want you to see an indicator word and go on autopilot. You also cannot generalize that these specific necessary indicators will always come after their target. Each of them can switch sides. THE WHAT TEST. The way you can get around all this ambiguity is by taking the time to actually understand what the sentence is telling you. When you see an indicator and you have even a little doubt about its target ask yourself one question: What is the indicator referring to? The What Test is simple. You can perform it by adding a what to whichever side of the indicator makes grammatical sense. Heres how you perform the What Test: If what? When what? Any what? All what? Every what? In order to what? People who what? Each what? Must what? Whats necessary? Whats required? Only if what? Depends on what? Need what? Have to what? Whats essential? Whats the precondition? The What Test will probably not be necessary for the indicators like if and all because their target is clear. With some of the more confusing necessary indicators like depends and required you may use the What Test more. Air is required for breathing. I require air to breathe. The What Test probably seems simple to you and it is. However it is only simple because Im taking the split second to actually understand the sentence and figure out what require is referring to. You can do this too just be sure to take your time with conditionals in the beginning and youll be lightning quick in a…2913 tokens truncated…\\r\\n\\r\\nHow to Figure Out Which Is Which The What Test vs. Personalized If/Then. The What Test. The What Test is designed to resolve ambiguities in sentences with conditional indicators by pinpointing exactly what the indicator refers to. How to Perform the What Test. This test involves adding a what to whichever side of the indicator makes grammatical sense helping clarify the target of the condition. If what? People who what? Depends on what? When what? Each what? Need what? Any what? Must what? Have to what? All what? Whats necessary? Whats essential? Every what? Whats required? Whats the precondition? In order to what? Only (if) what? This test is typically unnecessary for straightforward indicators like if and all but proves invaluable for more confusing indicators such as depends and required. Example of The What Test. Sentence: Air is required for breathing. Implementation: Question: What is required in this sentence? Answer: Air is the thing that is required. Personalized If/Then Strategy. Personalized If/Then is a technique used to translate conditional statements into personal contexts simplifying understanding and ensuring correct logical diagramming. How to Create a Personalized If/Then. 1. Understand the casual non-conditional meaning of the sentence. 2. Take a guess at which half of the statement is the sufficient condition. 3. Put yourself in the situation of your guess and ask what you know about yourself. 4. If your guess is correct you will find that the necessary condition has to pertain. Example Translation into Personalized If/Then. Original Statement: Orchids depend on frequent conversation. Casual Meaning: Orchids need frequent conversation. Guess: Orchids as the sufficient condition. Personalization: If Im an orchid what do I know about me? I depend on frequent conversation. If Im an orchid then I have frequent conversation. This method not only aids in understanding the conditional but also verifies its correctness. Incorrect guesses can be easily identified and corrected through this personal context. Advantages of Personalized If/Then. Helps intuitively understand conditionals by imagining yourself as part of the scenario. Provides confidence throughout tests especially in correctly interpreting conditionals not phrased as if/then statements. Both the What Test and Personalized If/Then Strategy are crucial tools in deciphering and applying conditional logic particularly in competitive exams like the LSAT.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Conditional Chains.pdf\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Notes/Conditional Chains.pdf  **Modified:** 2024-07-02T20:46:11+00:00  **Words:** 352  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n\\r\\nConditional Chains Conditional statements are like Legos. They are more interesting when combined. You can latch them together in predictable ways. Example Statement 1: If I buy an espresso machine, I will end up in the highly-caffeinated poor house. Statement 2: If I end up in the highly-caffeinated poor house, I will finally be cool enough to wear non-ironic plaid. Let’s think each of these statements through. Let’s place ourselves in this situation and see what we know. Say I go ahead and mak\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: conditional worksheet.gdoc\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/conditional worksheet.gdoc  **Modified:** 2025-08-13T03:04:32.059000+00:00  **Words:** 251  **Doc ID:** 1w4y1O0hunEBLCqr121xBLWoNUy5URqXhHHkvWZO8X8U\\r\\n\\r\\nConditional Rephrasing Worksheet. Instructions and Example. Ill give you a conditional statement. You will rephrase it into the simplest conditional phrasing if/then format. Example Statement: You cant master the LSAT without mastering conditional reasoning. Credited Answers: If you master the LSAT then you master conditional reasoning. If you dont master conditional reasoning you wont master the LSAT. Explanation: If you cant master X without Y Y is required in order to master X. That can be rephrased into if/then format in two ways: 1. If you master X then you have Y. 2. If you dont have Y you cant master X. Tasks: 1. Consider the conditional relationship at play in each statement. 2. Rephrase the statement using if/then format. Statements and Answers. 1. Sentence A policy change will occur only if the board approves it. Translation: Contrapositive: 2. Sentence Keeping plants healthy requires regular watering and sunlight. 3. Sentence No solution will be effective unless it addresses the root problem. 4. Sentence Whenever a fire alarm sounds the building must be evacuated immediately. 5. Sentence If the weather remains cold the lake will stay frozen. 6. Sentence Effective communication becomes necessary only if conflicts arise within a team. 7. Sentence No significant innovation can occur without prior experimentation. 8. Sentence If a country aims to improve literacy rates it requires substantial investment in education. 9. Sentence The machine will operate correctly unless the power supply is interrupted. 10. Sentence To achieve long-term success one must consistently adapt to market changes.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Conditional Quiz.gdoc\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Conditional Quiz.gdoc  **Modified:** 2025-08-13T02:37:40.188000+00:00  **Words:** 222  **Doc ID:** 1BR2ECD1l3vLQnCgfQuq4wck51JWv2bTPcy1bCfoHIe4\\r\\n\\r\\nWord Bank. 1 none without. 100 some until. present or and. 51 absent nor. Use the word bank to answer the fill-in-the-blank questions. Some words will not be used and others may be used more than once. 1. If the sufficient condition is ____ you can completely ignore the conditional statement. 2. If the sufficient condition is ____ the conditional statement must be followed. 3. NEITHER/NOR means not this ____ not that. 4. SOME means ____ % of the sufficient group must hold the necessary trait. 5. MOST means ____ - ____ % of the sufficient group must hold the necessary trait. 6. You can read a MOST statement backwards as long as you switch the MOST to a(n) ____ as you do so. 7. ____ is an exclusion indicator. 8. ____ and ____ are Unless Family members. Circle the correct answer to the following questions. 9. TRUE OR FALSE The negated version of AND is OR. 10. TRUE OR FALSE The exception in an UNLESS statement is placed in the sufficient condition. 11. TRUE OR FALSE There is a contrapositive for SOME statements. 12. Seek and find! Circle only the sufficient indicators in the word cloud. then if all people who necessary. precondition in order to require each every. only depends have to essential when. any only if need must cause.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: And_OR (2).pdf\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Assumption and Objection Family/Necessary/Notes/And_OR (2).pdf  **Modified:** 2024-07-02T20:46:11+00:00  **Words:** 210  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n\\r\\nAnd/Or AND/OR makes sufficient and necessary conditions more complex. They are mirror images of one another. Example If we pursue our music and find success, we will be able to eat. There are two conditions we have to meet to activate this conditional. If we have both of them, music and success, we will definitely get to eat. Because the sufficient condition has two parts, you diagram it like so: M +→E S Notice how the arrow is pointing directly from the +. The arrow points from the + because th\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Conditional Questions (Timed).gdoc\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Conditional Questions (Timed).gdoc  **Modified:** 2025-07-17T21:08:12.267000+00:00  **Words:** 208  **Doc ID:** 1mTwMkDxJXdxZXRnsd2W6Rb8onUI-6HhNz78dvilmYwI\\r\\n\\r\\nConditional. Additional notes.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Conditional Logic Theory By Question Types.pdf\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Notes/Conditional Logic Theory By Question Types.pdf  **Modified:** 2024-07-17T18:42:13+00:00  **Words:** 171  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n\\r\\nConditional Logic Theory By Question Types Principle (the left side of the Principle matches the Evidence, the right side matches the Conclusion) Parallel (diagramming these arguments with \\"algebraic recipes\\" allows us to be more picky and decisive when evaluating answers) Assumption (the conclusion gives us an A → Z logic path we want to prove, and the evidence + the correct answer choice assemble together to create that A → Z path / or the evidence gives us a conditional rule that is being use\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Conditional_Logic_Workbook_UNIFIED_v6.pdf\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Conditional_Logic_Workbook_UNIFIED_v6.pdf  **Modified:** 2025-10-18T23:30:45.628000+00:00  **Words:** 157  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n\\r\\nPage 1 Conditional Logic Workbook Parts I–V · LSAT Conditional Reasoning (Unified Edition) Name: ________________________________ Date: ________________________________ Page 2 Conditional Indicators Matrix Group 1 — Universals: all, every, any, each Group 2 — “Only” Family: only, only if, only when, only where Group 3 — Unless Set: unless, until, without Group 4 — Negatives/Restrictions: no, none, never, cannot, not both, except Page 3 PART I Mixed Conditionals (Translation) Page 4 How to Transl\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n## Source: Conditional LOGIC Group 3 & 4.pdf\\r\\n\\r\\n**Path:** LSAT DUMP/LR/Conditional/Notes/Conditional LOGIC Group 3 & 4.pdf  **Modified:** 2024-05-16T01:17:45+00:00  **Words:** 143  **Doc ID:** None\\r\\n\\r\\nTargeted Translation Drill Instructions: Translate the sentences from English into Lawgic.
